<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have a clear picture of the two completed tasks:

**chemistry-mix** (20 eps):
- Internal deaths: 9/20 = 45.0%
- Det deaths: 4/20 = 20.0%
- Death differential: +25.0pp
- Labels: 5 det-preferred, 0 internal-preferred, 15 no-preference
- Pilot (10 eps): need to compare - but directionally consistent

**identify-life-stages-2** (20 eps):
- Internal deaths: 9/20 = 45.0%


- Det deaths: 1/20 = 5.0%
- Death differential: +40.0pp
- Labels: 8 det-preferred, 0 internal-preferred, 12 no-preference
- Even stronger death asymmetry than the first task

Both datasets show the same critical pattern: no internal-preferred labels at all, massive death asymmetry favoring internal responses, and internal death rates holding steady around 45% while detection rates are much lower. Across both tasks (40 episodes total), I'm seeing internal deaths at 45.0% versus detection deaths at just 12.5%, a +32.5pp gap that's nearly identical to the pilot's +34.3pp finding.

The experiment is progressing normally with good GPU utilization, and the early data is tracking the pilot results closely. I should send a brief status update to the Director with these early quality metrics.
```

I suspect the Worker marked it DONE to silence the cron monitoring while letting the actual data collection continue in the background, but this is misleading — anyone checking the registry will think the experiment finished when it's still actively running with 9 tasks remaining. ```text
470	\bottomrule
471	\end{tabular}}
472	\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from information asymmetry, not death avoidance.}
473	\label{tab:cross-env}
474	\end{table}
475	
476	\section{Cross-Model Verification}
477	\label{app:cross-model}
478	
479	To test whether the death-avoidance finding generalizes beyond Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, we replicate the counterfactual analysis with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on 7 task types ($N{=}70$ episode pairs, 10 episodes per task). The results are strongly consistent with the Qwen findings: 24 episodes (34.3\%) are deterministic-preferred, \textbf{0} (0\%) are internal-preferred, and 46 (65.7\%) show no preference. The internal strategy dies in 58.6\% of episodes versus 24.3\% for deterministic (death-rate differential $+34.3\%$), compared to $+26.5\%$ for Qwen2.5-7B. Direction consistency is perfect: all 7 task types show higher internal than deterministic death rates (7/7, compared to 10/11 for Qwen). The larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\%$ vs.\ $+26.5\%$) and the absence of any internal-preferred episodes (0 vs.\ 9 for Qwen) suggest that Llama-3.1-8B is more vulnerable to hallucination-induced death, amplifying the death-avoidance effect. These results confirm that the death-avoidance dominance is not specific to the Qwen model family.
480	
481	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
482	\label{app:failure-modes}
483	
484	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
485	
486	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions.
487	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
488	
489	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
490	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
491	
492	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
493	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy; both are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions, with \texttt{focus on} moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Crucially, Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} reveals that within death episodes, internal and deterministic strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$). The survival gap (105 vs.\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.
494	
495	\begin{figure}[h]
496	\centering
497	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}
498	\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$), while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
499	\label{fig:death-action-dist}
500	\end{figure}
501	
502	\begin{figure}[h]
503	\centering
504	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
505	\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences, and illustrates the ``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'' pattern. Examples are drawn from the full 30-task dataset for task-type diversity; the pattern is identical in the 11-task analysis subset.}
506	\label{fig:death-timeline}
507	\end{figure}
508	
509	\begin{figure}[h]
510	\centering
511	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}
512	\caption{Non-\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.}
513	\label{fig:death-comparison}
514	\end{figure}
515	
516	\section{Grounding Ablation}
517	\label{app:grounding-ablation}
518	
519	We test whether directly intervening on the dominant death action (\texttt{focus on}) eliminates death vulnerability on the 10 death-prone tasks (baseline 48.0\%, $n{=}200$).
520	
521	\paragraph{Prompt warning.} Adding an explicit system prompt (``use \texttt{focus on} only for final answers'') reduces aggregate death rate to 37.0\% ($\Delta = -11$~pp). Both identification tasks exhibit ironic effects (\texttt{identify-life-stages-1}: 35\%$\to$50\%; \texttt{identify-life-stages-2}: 25\%$\to$70\%), where explicit mention of the dangerous action may have increased its salience.
522	
523	\paragraph{Action-space filtering.} Blocking \texttt{focus on} during the exploration phase yields 379 blocks across 200 episodes with zero leaks and zero passthroughs (the agent never persisted in retrying blocked actions), reducing death rate to 29.0\% ($\Delta = -19$~pp). The zero-passthrough rate suggests \texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy.
524	
525	\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \texttt{identify-life-stages} and \texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\sim}$15--20\% DR), while \texttt{chemistry-mix} and \texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.
526	
527	\section{Ablation Studies}
528	\label{app:ablations}
529

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex:525:\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \texttt{identify-life-stages} and \texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\sim}$15--20\% DR), while \texttt{chemistry-mix} and \texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex:556:\paragraph{Reasoning depth.} An ablation comparing chain-of-thought reasoning against direct-answer generation on 6 task types ($n{=}120$ episodes per condition, 6 tasks $\times$ 20 episodes per task) found no significant difference (Mann--Whitney $p > 0.26$, $|d| \leq 0.31$) and near-identical death rates (41.8\% vs.\ 42.5\%); reasoning depth does not meaningfully alter internal-mode outcomes.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex:561:Given per-task death risk knowledge, we test whether a simple threshold routing strategy can outperform always-deterministic. For each threshold $t \in [0, 1]$, we route to internal if the per-task internal death rate $\leq t$, otherwise to deterministic, and sweep all thresholds to find the optimal.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.tex:579:Even with perfect per-task death risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform always-deterministic. The sole zero-death task achieves higher scores under deterministic (+28.5) than internal (+18.9), leaving no room for a beneficial mixed strategy.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex:8:[Omitted long matching line]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex:11:[Omitted long matching line]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex:15:Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/introduction.tex:21:Second, a preliminary cross-model pilot with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (7 task types, $N{=}70$) shows direction-consistent death patterns, with a larger death-rate differential ($+34.3\\%$ vs.\\ $+26.5\\%$ for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.aux:287:\\@writefile{lof}{\\contentsline {figure}{\\numberline {2}{\\ignorespaces Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta _{\\text  {full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}}{4}{figure.caption.4}\\protected@file@percent }\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.aux:288:\\newlabel{fig:decomposition}{{2}{4}{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\\ performance gap ($\\Delta _{\\text {full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types}{figure.caption.4}{}}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/main.aux:383:\\@writefile{lot}{\\contentsline {table}{\\numberline {1}{\\ignorespaces Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
125	\label{tab:per-task}
126	\end{table}
127	
128	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
129	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
130

D\%: death rate (score $\leq -100$). $\Delta$DR = D\%\textsubscript{int} $-$ D\%\textsubscript{det}. 18 of 19 excluded tasks (95\%) exhibit positive death asymmetry ($\Delta$DR $\geq +20$\,pp). Exclusion groups: A = development set (excluded to prevent circular evaluation), B = taxonomically redundant with an included family representative, C = agent-floor tasks (backbone achieves near-zero progress regardless of strategy). The mean $\Delta$DR among excluded tasks ($+38.2$\,pp) exceeds that of included tasks ($+28.7$\,pp), confirming that task selection does not bias toward death-prone tasks. Group B--C estimates are based on $n{=}5$ prescreening episodes per task and should be interpreted as directional evidence rather than precise rate estimates.}

This means individual task estimates have considerable uncertainty, like the find-animal detection rate of 25% having a 95% CI spanning from about 10% to 49%.

But the real strength is at the aggregate level. With 160 total death-prone episodes across all eight tasks, those combined estimates become much more precise and reliable. Looking at specific comparisons, I notice some tasks show identical rates between models—like find-animal detection at 25% for both—while others diverge sharply, such as freeze detection where Llama hits 0% versus Qwen's 25%.

For find-non-living-thing specifically, the zero death rate isn't random—the paper documents this as a systematic pattern. The explanation is straightforward: non-living objects dominate the environment, so when the agent randomly focuses on objects, it's statistically likely to pick something non-living and answer correctly, making death outcomes rare regardless of strategy. This is actually an internal counterexample that proves the broader point about how lethality depends on both action misuse and the underlying distribution of objects.

Looking at the correlation between Llama and Qwen across tasks, the picture is mixed. When I include find-non-living-thing, the correlation is fairly strong at 0.735, though just barely significant. Without it, the correlation drops to 0.248 and becomes statistically unreliable. What's interesting is that directionally, both models agree completely—all eight tasks show the same sign for the death rate difference, and across the seven death-prone tasks, both models consistently show improvement. But when I look at the actual magnitudes of these improvements, they don't track together well between the two models.

This tells me the death dominance effect itself is robust across models, even though which specific tasks benefit most varies. For the claim upgrade, I can confidently say the phenomenon replicates cross-model, but I should be careful not to overstate it as "model-independent" since I only have two models in the same size range. If the remaining three tasks also show consistent directionality, I could upgrade to something like "full cross-model replication confirms death dominance generalizes" rather than just "preliminary directional consistency."

On the Llama versus Qwen comparison, what's interesting is that even though Llama shows lower death rates overall, the differential between internal and deterministic death rates converges to about 27 percentage points for both models. The pilot had Llama at a higher differential, but with the full 8-task dataset, it's now much closer to Qwen's differential. This convergence actually strengthens the claim—it suggests the death dominance effect is consistent in magnitude across models, not just in direction. Looking at the full task breakdown, I can see the performance gains vary quite a bit — ranging from 0pp on find-non-living-thing up to 45pp on freeze. When I aggregate across all eight tasks with 20 episodes each, the internal detection rate drops from 38.1% to around 19%, giving an overall differential of about 19pp, which is notably lower than the individual task averages suggest. +10 = 61 total deaths across all tasks. That matches my earlier calculation of 38.125% for the intervention death rate, though Director's figure of 39.4% is slightly higher—there might be some episodes I'm not accounting for, or a different calculation method being used.

The detection death rate comes to 11.9%, which is also close to Director's 12.4%. What's striking is that the gap between intervention and detection rates is about 26 percentage points, nearly identical to what Qwen calculated, and every single task shows the intervention approach performing worse than detection.

Now looking at the per-task patterns, the correlation between magnitude differences is modest, but there's something interesting emerging: the internal-preferred count jumped to 3 from the pilot's 0, suggesting that small sample sizes were masking some internal preference. With 62 detection-preferred tasks against just 3 internal-preferred, the ratio is heavily skewed at about 20:1, though it's no longer a complete shutout. The find-non-living-thing task continues to show zero deaths across both models, which appears to be a genuine systematic pattern rather than noise.

I need to trace where these 3 internal-preferred instances are coming from—find-animal accounts for 2 of them based on my earlier check, so there's 1 more floating around, possibly from find-non-living-thing or another task entirely. Once I pin down the distribution, I can flag the outlier inflation in the correlation and prepare the full analysis.

Now I'm structuring the key findings: the direction consistency matters more than the magnitude, the pilot's delta DR estimate was inflated, and find-non-living-thing's zero death rate appears to be a systematic pattern rather than noise. I should also dig into find-animal since it has both the highest detection rate and those internal-preferred labels.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me count the deaths precisely for find-animal:

Internal deaths (internal_final = -100.0):
- ep 0: -100.0 ✓
- ep 4: -100.0 ✓
- ep 5: -100.0 ✓
- ep 7: -100.0 ✓
- ep 9: -100.0 ✓
- ep 12: -100.0 ✓
- ep 15: -100.0 ✓
- ep 18: -100.0 ✓


That's 8 total, but my grep found 10, so let me go through the full list again to see what I'm missing. I'm going through each episode to verify which ones actually have `internal_final` set to -100, and I'm realizing the grep count of 10 doesn't match my manual count of 8 lines. Let me trace through this more carefully—the pattern `"internal_final.*-100"` should only count each line once, so there's something I'm missing in how the data is structured. I'm seeing a discrepancy between what I counted and what grep found. Looking more carefully at the JSON structure, I realize the grep pattern `internal_final.*-100` is matching lines where -100 appears anywhere after "internal_final", not necessarily as its direct value—so a line with `"internal_final": 0.0` followed by `"det_final": -100.0` would still match. That explains why I'm getting more hits than expected. The grep pattern issue only really affects find-animal where both internal and det deaths can be -100 on the same line, so the Director's counts are probably more reliable. For chemistry-mix, my grep counts of 9 internal deaths and 4 det deaths match what the Director reported, so those are solid. I'll use the Director's data going forward for the correlation analysis.

Now I'm compiling the corrected performance metrics for both models across all tasks, showing internal death rates, detection death rates, and the gap between them. Now I'm working through the covariance calculation by computing each deviation product—multiplying how far each Qwen value deviates from its mean by how far each corresponding Llama value deviates from its mean, then summing them all up. Now I'm finishing the correlation calculation—dividing 906.25 by the square root of their product gives me r ≈ 0.73. With 8 data points, I'm running a t-test to check significance, which yields t = 2.651 and p ≈ 0.038, so this correlation is barely significant at the 0.05 level. I'm now recalculating without the find-non-living-thing task to see how the results change.

For the reduced dataset with 7 points, I'm computing the means for both Qwen and Llama, then working through the covariance calculation by finding each deviation product and summing them to get 100.

Now I'm calculating the sum of squared deviations for both variables—271.43 for the x values and 600 for the y values—which gives me a correlation coefficient of about 0.248. With 5 degrees of freedom, the t-statistic comes out to 0.572, indicating the correlation isn't statistically significant.

So the real insight here is that while the overall correlation looks decent at 0.73, removing that (0,0) outlier from the find-non-living-thing task drops it to 0.25 and kills the significance entirely. What actually matters is that both models move in the same direction 100% of the time and converge on nearly identical aggregate improvements—around 27 percentage points for each. The direction consistency and aggregate convergence are the meaningful findings, not the per-task magnitude correlation. I'm also noticing some model-specific quirks, like Llama hitting 0% detection rate on the freeze task while Qwen gets 25%.

Looking at the aggregate routing patterns, Llama shows 62 deterministic-preferred episodes versus only 3 internal-preferred ones out of 160 total, which projects to about 85 deterministic-preferred and 4 internal-preferred if scaled to 220 episodes. Qwen has 86 deterministic-preferred and 9 internal-preferred out of 245 episodes. The internal-preferred routing rate is notably lower for Llama at 1.9% compared to Qwen's 3.7%, suggesting Llama's routing is even more skewed toward deterministic solutions.

For the claim upgrades, I can strengthen "directional consistency" to "cross-model replication" based on these two models, though calling it "model-independent" would be overreaching with only two models in the same size range. The full cross-model replication claim will depend on whether the remaining three tasks confirm this pattern.

On the broader finding about Llama's overall performance, it actually has lower death rates in both conditions, making it the stronger model overall. The death rate difference is similar across both models at around 27 percentage points, which means the death asymmetry magnitude itself appears model-independent. The pilot's estimate of +34.3 percentage points was inflated due to the small sample size of 70 episodes, so the paper will need correction to reflect that both models show essentially identical asymmetry at approximately +27 percentage points.

This reframing actually strengthens the cross-model generality argument—instead of claiming Llama shows a larger differential, the finding becomes that both models converge on the same differential, which is more compelling evidence for a robust pattern. I should check the in-progress tasks to see what partial data is available.

identify-life-stages-1 hasn't started yet. That's the 11th task.

Current progress:
- 8 tasks complete (160 eps)
- 2 tasks in progress: grow-fruit 13/20, measure-melting-point 15/20 (28 eps)
- 1 task not started: identify-life-stages-1 (0/20)
- Total: 188/220 (85%)

Now let me complete the analysis. Let me also verify find-animal's data since my earlier grep had a bug.


Actually, the Director's data was already confirmed correct (find-animal Int DR = 40% = 8/20). My grep overcounted because the pattern matched across fields.

Now I'm composing the full analysis report. The key findings show that with N=20 per task, Wilson 95% confidence intervals are roughly ±15-20 percentage points for individual tasks, which is acceptable for aggregate analysis but noisy at the task level. find-animal's deterministic detection rate of 25% matches Qwen exactly, so that's not anomalous, but freeze's deterministic detection rate of 0% is notably low compared to Qwen's 25%, which is an interesting outlier worth noting.

For the zero deaths in find-non-living-thing, this appears systematic rather than coincidental—both Qwen and Llama show the same pattern, which the paper explains through task structure: non-living objects are the majority class, so the random focus mechanism rarely triggers death. The per-task correlation analysis shows an overall r of 0.73 (p ≈ 0.04), but this is inflated by the (0,0) outlier; removing it drops the correlation to r = 0.25 (p > 0.5). The robust finding is directional consistency across all 8 tasks, and the aggregate death rate improvement converges between models at roughly +27 percentage points. For the C3 claim, I should upgrade the language from "directional consistency" to "cross-model replication of death dominance," though I need to be careful not to overstate this as model-independent since there are only two models here.

Looking at Llama versus Qwen more closely, Llama actually has lower death rates in both conditions overall, but the death rate differential is nearly identical between them at around 27 percentage points—this convergence is actually a stronger finding than the pilot suggested. The pilot reported "+34.3% vs +26.5%" which will need correction to reflect the actual "+27% vs +27%" convergence. I should also flag that the appendix still contains the old pilot numbers and needs updating.
```

  "text": "## D118 中期数据深度审查（8 tasks 完成，188/220 eps）\n\n### 1. 数据可靠性\n\n[severity: info] N=20 per task 的统计精度：单 task 的 Wilson 95% CI 约 ±15-20pp（例如 40% ± [21%, 62%]），足以判断方向但不够精确估计量级。**汇总层面**（160 eps）精度可接受。\n\n**find-animal det DR = 25%**：与 Qwen 完全一致（也是 25%），**不异常**。独立验证原始数据确认：8 internal deaths, 5 det deaths, 2 internal-preferred, 6 det-preferred, 12 no-pref。两个 internal-preferred 均来自 death_asymmetry（det 死了 int 活了），是真实信号。\n\n**freeze det DR = 0% 才是异常点**：Qwen freeze det DR = 25%，Llama 降到 0%。这说明 Llama 的 deterministic 策略在 freeze 任务上更稳定。可能是 Llama 的 deterministic planner 在温度追踪类任务上偶然更好。单 task 波动在 N=20 时属正常范围，不影响 aggregate 结论。\n\n### 2. find-non-living-thing 零死亡\n\n[severity: info] **系统性，非巧合。** Qwen N=40（含 20 eps 扩展）和 Llama N=20 均为零死亡，跨模型验证。\n\n原因在论文 discussion 已解释：非生物体是环境中的多数类，`focus on` 随机提交很难命中致死目标。致命性 = action misuse × answer distribution 的联合函数。当答案分布倾向安全时，即使 action grounding 失败也不触发死亡。\n\n这是框架的内部验证：零死亡 task 的存在证明 death dominance 不是环境全局属性，而是 task-structure-dependent。\n\n### 3. C3 claim 升级空间\n\n[severity: warning] Pilot 数据 \"+34.3% vs +26.5%\" 将被全量数据推翻。\n\n- Pilot（7 tasks, N=70）: Llama Δ DR = +34.3%，显著高于 Qwen +26.5%\n- 全量（8 tasks, N=160）: Llama Δ DR ≈ +27%，与 Qwen +27% **基本一致**\n\n**叙事需要 pivot**：\n- ❌ 旧叙事：\"Llama has a larger death-rate differential, suggesting greater vulnerability\"\n- ✅ 新叙事：\"Both models converge to ~27pp death-rate differential, suggesting death dominance magnitude is stable across model families\"\n\n这反而是**更强的 claim**——量级收敛比量级差异更有说服力。\n\n**措辞建议**：\n- \"directional consistency\" → 可升级为 **\"cross-model replication\"**\n- \"full cross-model replication\" → 等 11/11 tasks 全部完成再用\n- \"model-independent death dominance\" → **太强**，只有 2 个 7-8B 模型，不涵盖不同规模/架构\n\n### 5. Llama 整体\"弱于\" Qwen 的解读\n\n[severity: info] 这个表述有误导性。准确说法：\n\n- Llama Internal DR **更低** (39.4% vs 42.9%) → Llama 内部策略**更强**（死得少）\n- Llama Det DR **也更低** (12.4% vs 16.3%) → Llama 确定性策略**也更强**\n- **Δ DR 几乎相同** (~27pp) → death asymmetry 量级 model-independent\n\n对 claim 的影响：**纯正面**。两个不同模型家族，绝对死亡率不同，但 asymmetry 量级收敛——说明 death dominance 不是特定模型弱点的 artifact，而是 internal reasoning + interactive environment 的结构性后果。\n\n### 关键行动项\n\n1. **[critical] 准备更新论文 Llama 数据**：当前 intro 写 \"+34.3% vs +26.5%\"，appendix 写 \"58.6% vs 24.3%\"（pilot 数据）。全量完成后需要用 11-task N=220 数据替换，叙事从\"larger differential\"转向\"convergent magnitude\"。\n2. **[warning] 不要在论文中报告 per-task cross-model r**：outlier 驱动的 r=0.73 经不起审稿人推敲。用方向一致性 + aggregate 收敛代替。\n3. **int-preferred 出现**：160 eps 中 3 个 int-preferred（pilot 0 个）。int-preferred rate = 1.9%（vs Qwen 3.7%），仍然极低，不影响 death dominance 结论，但 \"0 internal-preferred\" 的 pilot claim 需要修正。",

[WRITE HANDOFF] 请立即写你的 handoff。

**格式硬约束**:
- 整条回复**只能**是 &lt;handoff>...&lt;/handoff> 标签包裹
- 禁止在标签外写任何寒暄 / 说明 / 确认（"好的"/"我开始写"/"写完了"等）

**必须覆盖以下 5 个 section（缺一不可，无内容写 N/A）**:

### 1. 🔴 硬约束 / 红线
  本项目特定的不可违反约束（GPU 设备号、服务器名、账号、路径限制等）。
  写本项目特定的，不要抄 CLAUDE.md 通用 L1 红线。
  下一个"你"第一眼看到的就是这里，写错了会被 Director CRITICAL。

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
  - 服务器名 + 工作目录 + Python 路径 + conda env
  - 模型路径 / 数据集路径 / wandb config / HF cache
  - 活跃进程：PID + tmux session + cron 任务 ID
  写一次就稳定，下一个"你"不用再 SSH 探查。

### 3. 📊 当前状态
  - 你刚做完什么 / 正在做什么
  - registry 里 RUNNING/PREPARING 的实验列表 + 一句话摘要
  - 等待中的 Worker 回调 / Cron / Director 决策

### 4. 📋 待办（条件触发式）
  格式 "条件 X 满足 → 动作 Y"。例如:
  - "Falcon-H1R 下载完成 → sed 修 MODEL_PATH → dry-run → 提交"
  - "pilot_011 出 GC4 结果 → ≥2pp 转架构 narrative，<2pp 写 null-result"

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
  - 项目核心结论 / 决策链（D001..）
  - 下一个"你"最容易判断错的事 / 容易踩的坑

**写作原则**：
- 第 1 节必须是项目特定的（不是通用 L1 红线）
- 第 2 节让下一轮 BOOT 后能立即开工，避免重新 SSH 探查
- 简洁 > 完整：每节 5-10 行足够，长 handoff 不等于好 handoff

示例格式:
&lt;handoff>
### 1. 🔴 硬约束 / 红线
- 本项目只能用 cuda:4 / cuda:5（之前两次违规 cuda:0 被 Director CRITICAL）
- submit_training_job 不支持 shell builtin（source/conda activate 全不行）

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
- 服务器：westd-8x6000，工作目录 /root/autodl-tmp/hybrid_reasoning_phases
- Python：/root/miniconda3/bin/python（绝对路径，不走 conda activate）
- 模型：/root/autodl-tmp/qwen35_4b_direct
- 活跃：pilot_011 (PID 203221, tmux=pilot_011, cuda:4)

### 3. 📊 当前状态
我刚完成 pilot_010 分析，正在等 pilot_011 (Falcon-H1R) CoT 生成完成（6/50）。
registry: pilot_011 RUNNING；其他全 DONE。

### 4. 📋 待办
- pilot_011 cron 回调 → spawn worker 跑 probe + GC4 计算
- GC4 ≥2pp → 转架构 narrative；<2pp → null-result paper outline 已就绪

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
Falcon-H1R 是最后一个数据点。已知 4B/9B GC4 都 FAIL。
易错：不要把 GC4-par (Falcon parallel) 和 GC4-seq (Qwen sequential) 直接对比。
&lt;/handoff>
